the wrong measurements or part numbers on estimates, like writing 36 instead of 63 or missing a line on the permit form, and then I only catch it after the lumber is ordered or somebody has to redo (Note: In adolescents or adults, may be limited to feeling restless.)\n  manifestation: If I have to sit at the desk doing estimates or sit through a long meeting, I feel wound up inside like I need to get up and move around, even though my wrist is healed and I know I’m supposed to just stay put and finish.\n- key: S013\n  name: Quiet_Activity_Difficulty\n  description: Often unable to play or engage in leisure activities quietly.\n  manifestation: Even at home or in church, when things are supposed to be quiet, I catch myself tapping, shifting around, whispering to my husband, or getting up for no real reason because sitting still in the quiet feels almost unbearable.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: Onset prior to age 12 years\n  manifestation: Even back in grade school, before I was 12, teachers were always telling my mother I was bright but careless, losing homework, forgetting instructions, and staring out the window instead of finishing my seatwork.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: My mistakes and delays have cost me commissions, caused rework on jobs, and made the crew less willing to rely on me for scheduling or ordering. Even when I know I’m supposed to just stay put and finish. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Did you have difficulty paying attention or sitting still in school when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Yes… I think it has, unfortunately. I don’t like saying that, because I do try hard and I don’t want to sound careless, but it has caused problems.

And at home… my husband has had to take over a lot of the bills and reminders, because I misplace papers or miss what needs to be done. I feel bad about that. I’m not trying to put it on him. It just… keeps happening. Does that answer what you mean?

I mean, I can make quick decisions sometimes, but I wouldn’t say I’m the kind of person who just goes and does things without thinking. At least, I don’t think so. If there’s a better way to judge that, you can tell me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I’m especially talkative… at least not more than other people. I might talk a bit if I’m comfortable or if it’s about work, but I don’t think people would say I dominate conversations or anything. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble completing tasks or projects once you've started them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I do finish things eventually, usually, but lately it’s been really hard to stay with one thing long enough to get it done. Like if I’m in the office doing paperwork or estimates, one saw starts up, or someone walks by, or I suddenly think of another job I need to check on, and I’m off doing that instead.\n\nThen I come back and I’ve lost my place, and… the whole day can kind of get away from me. I don’t want it to sound like I’m careless or anything, but that’s honestly a lot of what made me come in. I just can’t seem to keep my mind on one thing the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Were you more physically active or restless than your peers when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure about as a child, honestly… I don’t remember being the kid who couldn’t sit still more than everyone else. But now, if I have to sit at the desk doing estimates or sit through a long meeting, I get this sort of wound-up feeling inside, like I really need to get up and move around. Even when I know I’m supposed to just stay put and finish. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Did you have difficulty paying attention or sitting still in school when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it was, because I did okay in school, but even back in grade school—before I was 12—teachers would tell my mother I was bright, but kind of careless.\n\nI’d lose homework, or forget what instructions they’d just given, and I remember staring out the window instead of finishing my seatwork. My moods feel pretty tied to what’s going on, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Did your difficulty with attention and restlessness start when you were a child or did it develop later in life?\n\nPatient: I think it started when I was a kid, yes. I didn’t really see it that way then, and I still don’t want to overstate it, but even in grade school—before I was 12—teachers would tell my mother I was bright, just careless.\n\nI’d lose homework, forget instructions they’d just given, or end up staring out the window instead of finishing my work. So… I guess it wasn’t something that only came on later in life. Maybe I lose track of time a little here and there, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it. It’s both, I think… not just work. Maybe I lose track of time a little here and there, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with attention and restlessness caused you any problems in your relationships or daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has, unfortunately. I don’t like saying that, because I do try hard and I don’t want to sound careless, but it has caused problems.\n\nAt work, if I miss something or get behind, it can turn into rework on a job, and I’ve lost commissions because of mistakes or delays. The crew doesn’t always want to rely on me for things like scheduling or ordering, because I might forget a detail or not get it done when I meant to.\n\nAnd at home… my husband has had to take over a lot of the bills and reminders, because I misplace papers or miss what needs to be done. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with attention and restlessness at home, or is it mainly a problem at work?\n\nPatient: It’s both, I think… not just work. The crew doesn’t always want to rely on me for things like scheduling or ordering, because I might forget a detail or not get it done when I meant to.\n\nAnd at home… my husband has had to take over a lot of the bills and reminders, because I misplace papers or miss what needs to be done. My husband has had to take over a lot of the bills and reminders because I’ll misplace papers or miss what needs to be done. I don’t mean to make extra work for him, but… yes, it’s definitely not only at work.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had difficulty with attention and restlessness in both work and home settings, or has it varied over time?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there a long time, honestly… though I don’t want to make it sound like I was a terrible student or anything. I did okay in some ways, and people said I was bright.\n\nBut even back in grade school, before I was 12, teachers would tell my mother things like I was careless, or that I’d lose homework, forget instructions, or just be staring out the window instead of finishing my work. Yes… mostly with my husband, I’d say. I mean, he’s very patient with me, so I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but I know it wears on him.

Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself so focused on a task that you lose track of time and forget about everything else around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… not in a dramatic way, I don’t think.\n\nI can get absorbed in something, especially if I’m trying to do it right, but I don’t know that I forget the whole world or anything. Maybe I lose track of time a little here and there, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with attention and restlessness caused you any problems in your relationships or daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has, unfortunately. I don’t like saying that, because I do try hard and I don’t want to sound careless, but it has caused problems.\n\nAt work, if I miss something or get behind, it can turn into rework on a job, and I’ve lost commissions because of mistakes or delays. The crew doesn’t always want to rely on me for things like scheduling or ordering, because I might forget a detail or not get it done when I meant to.\n\nAnd at home… my husband has had to take over a lot of the bills and reminders, because I misplace papers or miss what needs to be done. I feel bad about that. I’m not trying to put it on him. It just… keeps happening. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with attention and restlessness at home, or is it mainly a problem at work?\n\nPatient: It’s both, I think… not just work. Work is where it shows up more obviously, because mistakes can cost money — like I’ve lost commissions, or caused rework on jobs when I miss something or get delayed. And I think the crew has gotten less willing to count on me for scheduling or ordering, which is hard to admit.\n\nBut at home it happens too. My husband has had to take over a lot of the bills and reminders because I’ll misplace papers or miss what needs to be done. I don’t mean to make extra work for him, but… yes, it’s definitely not only at work.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had difficulty with attention and restlessness in both work and home settings, or has it varied over time?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there a long time, honestly… though I don’t want to make it sound like I was a terrible student or anything. I did okay in some ways, and people said I was bright.\n\nBut even back in grade school, before I was 12, teachers would tell my mother things like I was careless, or that I’d lose homework, forget instructions, or just be staring out the window instead of finishing my work. So… I guess it wasn’t just something that started at work or at home as an adult. It’s kind of followed me, just showing up differently over time. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with attention and restlessness caused problems in your personal relationships, such as with your husband or friends?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly with my husband, I’d say. I mean, he’s very patient with me, so I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but I know it wears on him.\n\nHe’s had to take over bills and reminders because I keep misplacing papers or missing what needs to be done, and that’s not really fair to him. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Did you have difficulty paying attention or sitting still in school when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it was, because I did okay in school, but even back in grade school—before I was 12—teachers would tell my mother I was bright, but kind of careless.\n\nI’d lose homework, or forget what instructions they’d just given, and I remember staring out the window instead of finishing my seatwork. So… yes, I guess paying attention was a problem even then. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have strong mood swings, where you feel extremely happy, angry, or sad for no apparent reason?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I get frustrated or down like anyone does, especially when I’m overwhelmed, but I wouldn’t say I have these big swings where I’m suddenly extremely happy or angry or sad for no reason.\n\nAt least, I don’t think so. My moods feel pretty tied to what’s going on, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Did your difficulty with attention and restlessness start when you were a child or did it develop later in life?\n\nPatient: I think it started when I was a kid, yes. I didn’t really see it that way then, and I still don’t want to overstate it, but even in grade school—before I was 12—teachers would tell my mother I was bright, just careless.\n\nI’d lose homework, forget instructions they’d just given, or end up staring out the window instead of finishing my work. So… I guess it wasn’t something that only came on later in life. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself so focused on a task that you lose track of time and forget about everything else around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… not in a dramatic way, I don’t think.\n\nI can get absorbed in something, especially if I’m trying to do it right, but I don’t know that I forget the whole world or anything. Maybe I lose track of time a little here and there, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with attention and restlessness caused you any problems in your relationships or daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has, unfortunately. I don’t like saying that, because I do try hard and I don’t want to sound careless, but it has caused problems.\n\nAt work, if I miss something or get behind, it can turn into rework on a job, and I’ve lost commissions because of mistakes or delays. The crew doesn’t always want to rely on me for things like scheduling or ordering, because I might forget a detail or not get it done when I meant to.\n\nAnd at home… my husband has had to take over a lot of the bills and reminders, because I misplace papers or miss what needs to be done. I feel bad about that. I’m not trying to put it on him. It just… keeps happening. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with attention and restlessness at home, or is it mainly a problem at work?\n\nPatient: It’s both, I think… not just work. Work is where it shows up more obviously, because mistakes can cost money — like I’ve lost commissions, or caused rework on jobs when I miss something or get delayed. And I think the crew has gotten less willing to count on me for scheduling or ordering, which is hard to admit.\n\nBut at home it happens too. My husband has had to take over a lot of the bills and reminders because I’ll misplace papers or miss what needs to be done. I don’t mean to make extra work for him, but… yes, it’s definitely not only at work.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had difficulty with attention and restlessness in both work and home settings, or has it varied over time?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there a long time, honestly… though I don’t want to make it sound like I was a terrible student or anything. I did okay in some ways, and people said I was bright.\n\nBut even back in grade school, before I was 12, teachers would tell my mother things like I was careless, or that I’d lose homework, forget instructions, or just be staring out the window instead of finishing my work. So… I guess it wasn’t just something that started at work or at home as an adult. It’s kind of followed me, just showing up differently over time. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with attention and restlessness caused problems in your personal relationships, such as with your husband or friends?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly with my husband, I’d say. I mean, I can make quick decisions sometimes, but I wouldn’t say I’m the kind of person who just goes and does things without thinking. At least, I don’t think so. If there’s a better way to judge that, you can tell me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I’m especially talkative… at least not more than other people. I might talk a bit if I’m comfortable or if it’s about work, but I don’t think people would say I dominate conversations or anything. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble completing tasks or projects once you've started them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I do finish things eventually, usually, but lately it’s been really hard to stay with one thing long enough to get it done. Like if I’m in the office doing paperwork or estimates, one saw starts up, or someone walks by, or I suddenly think of another job I need to check on, and I’m off doing that instead.\n\nThen I come back and I’ve lost my place, and… the whole day can kind of get away from me. I don’t want it to sound like I’m careless or anything, but that’s honestly a lot of what made me come in. I just can’t seem to keep my mind on one thing the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Were you more physically active or restless than your peers when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure about as a child, honestly… I don’t remember being the kid who couldn’t sit still more than everyone else. But now, if I have to sit at the desk doing estimates or sit through a long meeting, I get this sort of wound-up feeling inside, like I really need to get up and move around. Even when I know I’m supposed to just stay put and finish. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Did you have difficulty paying attention or sitting still in school when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it was, because I did okay in school, but even back in grade school—before I was 12—teachers would tell my mother I was bright, but kind of careless.\n\nI’d lose homework, or forget what instructions they’d just given, and I remember staring out the window instead of finishing my seatwork. So… yes, I guess paying attention was a problem even then. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have strong mood swings, where you feel extremely happy, angry, or sad for no apparent reason?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I get frustrated or down like anyone does, especially when I’m overwhelmed, but I wouldn’t say I have these big swings where I’m suddenly extremely happy or angry or sad for no reason.\n\nAt least, I don’t think so. My moods feel pretty tied to what’s going on, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Did your difficulty with attention and restlessness start when you were a child or did it develop later in life?\n\nPatient: I think it started when I was a kid, yes. I didn’t really see it that way then, and I still don’t want to overstate it, but even in grade school—before I was 12—teachers would tell my mother I was bright, just careless.\n\nI’d lose homework, forget instructions they’d just given, or end up staring out the window instead of finishing my work. So… I guess it wasn’t something that only came on later in life. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself so focused on a task that you lose track of time and forget about everything else around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… not in a dramatic way, I don’t think.\n\nI can get absorbed in something, especially if I’m trying to do it right, but I don’t know that I forget the whole world or anything. Maybe I lose track of time a little here and there, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with attention and restlessness caused you any problems in your relationships or daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has, unfortunately. I don’t like saying that, because I do try hard and I don’t want to sound careless, but it has caused problems.\n\nAt work, if I miss something or get behind, it can turn into rework on a job, and I’ve lost commissions because of mistakes or delays. The crew doesn’t always want to rely on me for things like scheduling or ordering, because I might forget a detail or not get it done when I meant to.\n\nAnd at home… my husband has had to take over a lot of the bills and reminders, because I misplace papers or miss what needs to be done. I feel bad about that. I’m not trying to put it on him. It just… keeps happening. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with attention and restlessness at home, or is it mainly a problem at work?\n\nPatient: It’s both, I think… not just work. Work is where it shows up more obviously, because mistakes can cost money — like I’ve lost commissions, or caused rework on jobs when I miss something or get delayed. And I think the crew has gotten less willing to count on me for scheduling or ordering, which is hard to admit.\n\nBut at home it happens too. My husband has had to take over a lot of the bills and reminders because I’ll misplace papers or miss what needs to be done. I don’t mean to make extra work for him, but… yes, it’s definitely not only at work.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had difficulty with attention and restlessness in both work and home settings, or has it varied over time?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there a long time, honestly… though I don’t want to make it sound like I was a terrible student or anything. I did okay in some ways, and people said I was bright.\n\nBut even back in grade school, before I was 12, teachers would tell my mother things like I was careless, or that I’d lose homework, forget instructions, or just be staring out the window instead of finishing my work. So… I guess it wasn’t just something that started at work or at home as an adult. It’s kind of followed me, just showing up differently over time. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with attention and restlessness caused problems in your personal relationships, such as with your husband or friends?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly with my husband, I’d say. I mean, he’s very patient with me, so I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but I know it wears on him.\n\nHe’s had to take over bills and reminders because I keep misplacing papers or missing what needs to be done, and that’s not really fair to him. I feel embarrassed about it, honestly. With work it’s more obvious — mistakes and delays have cost me commissions or caused rework, and the crew doesn’t always trust me with scheduling or ordering now — but at home it does affect us too.\n\nI don’t think I’m trying to be difficult. It just keeps creating extra strain. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been fired or had to quit a job because of your difficulties with attention and restlessness?\n\nPatient: Not fired, no… not that I can say. And I don’t think I’ve had to quit because of it either.\n\nI’ve had some rough stretches at work, and I worry about how I’m seen, but I’ve managed to keep working. I hope that answers what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever become so focused on something that you can't stop thinking about it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think so, at least not in a way that feels like I can’t stop. I mean, if I’m working on something I care about, I might think about it for a while, but I wouldn’t say I get stuck on it. \n\nUnless you mean something more specific? I just don’t want to answer it wrong.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think so. I don’t want to overstate it, because I did get by, but it goes back a long way.

Even in grade school, before I was 12, teachers would tell my mother I was bright, but careless. I’d lose homework, forget instructions, or just be sitting there staring out the window instead of finishing my seatwork.

So yes, it wasn’t just recent or only at work. Does that answer what you mean?

Even when I know I’m supposed to just stay put and finish. I mean, I can make quick decisions sometimes, but I wouldn’t say I’m the kind of person who just goes and does things without thinking. At least, I don’t think so. I don’t think I’m especially talkative… at least not more than other people. I might talk a bit if I’m comfortable or if it’s about work, but I don’t think people would say I dominate conversations or anything. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble completing tasks or projects once you've started them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I do finish things eventually, usually, but lately it’s been really hard to stay with one thing long enough to get it done. Like if I’m in the office doing paperwork or estimates, one saw starts up, or someone walks by, or I suddenly think of another job I need to check on, and I’m off doing that instead.\n\nThen I come back and I’ve lost my place, and… the whole day can kind of get away from me. I don’t want it to sound like I’m careless or anything, but that’s honestly a lot of what made me come in. I just can’t seem to keep my mind on one thing the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Were you more physically active or restless than your peers when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure about as a child, honestly… I don’t remember being the kid who couldn’t sit still more than everyone else. But now, if I have to sit at the desk doing estimates or sit through a long meeting, I get this sort of wound-up feeling inside, like I really need to get up and move around. Even when I know I’m supposed to just stay put and finish. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Did you have difficulty paying attention or sitting still in school when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it was, because I did okay in school, but even back in grade school—before I was 12—teachers would tell my mother I was bright, but kind of careless.\n\nI’d lose homework, or forget what instructions they’d just given, and I remember staring out the window instead of finishing my seatwork. So… yes, I guess paying attention was a problem even then. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have strong mood swings, where you feel extremely happy, angry, or sad for no apparent reason?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I get frustrated or down like anyone does, especially when I’m overwhelmed, but I wouldn’t say I have these big swings where I’m suddenly extremely happy or angry or sad for no reason.\n\nAt least, I don’t think so. My moods feel pretty tied to what’s going on, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Did your difficulty with attention and restlessness start when you were a child or did it develop later in life?\n\nPatient: I think it started when I was a kid, yes. I didn’t really see it that way then, and I still don’t want to overstate it, but even in grade school—before I was 12—teachers would tell my mother I was bright, just careless.\n\nI’d lose homework, forget instructions they’d just given, or end up staring out the window instead of finishing my work. So… I guess it wasn’t something that only came on later in life. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself so focused on a task that you lose track of time and forget about everything else around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… not in a dramatic way, I don’t think.\n\nI can get absorbed in something, especially if I’m trying to do it right, but I don’t know that I forget the whole world or anything. Maybe I lose track of time a little here and there, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with attention and restlessness caused you any problems in your relationships or daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has, unfortunately. I don’t like saying that, because I do try hard and I don’t want to sound careless, but it has caused problems.\n\nAt work, if I miss something or get behind, it can turn into rework on a job, and I’ve lost commissions because of mistakes or delays. The crew doesn’t always want to rely on me for things like scheduling or ordering, because I might forget a detail or not get it done when I meant to.\n\nAnd at home… my husband has had to take over a lot of the bills and reminders, because I misplace papers or miss what needs to be done. I feel bad about that. I’m not trying to put it on him. It just… keeps happening. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with attention and restlessness at home, or is it mainly a problem at work?\n\nPatient: It’s both, I think… not just work. Work is where it shows up more obviously, because mistakes can cost money — like I’ve lost commissions, or caused rework on jobs when I miss something or get delayed. And I think the crew has gotten less willing to count on me for scheduling or ordering, which is hard to admit.\n\nBut at home it happens too. My husband has had to take over a lot of the bills and reminders because I’ll misplace papers or miss what needs to be done. I don’t mean to make extra work for him, but… yes, it’s definitely not only at work.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had difficulty with attention and restlessness in both work and home settings, or has it varied over time?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there a long time, honestly… though I don’t want to make it sound like I was a terrible student or anything. I did okay in some ways, and people said I was bright.\n\nBut even back in grade school, before I was 12, teachers would tell my mother things like I was careless, or that I’d lose homework, forget instructions, or just be staring out the window instead of finishing my work. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with attention and restlessness caused problems in your personal relationships, such as with your husband or friends?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly with my husband, I’d say. It just keeps creating extra strain. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been fired or had to quit a job because of your difficulties with attention and restlessness?\n\nPatient: Not fired, no… not that I can say. I hope that answers what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever become so focused on something that you can't stop thinking about it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think so, at least not in a way that feels like I can’t stop. I hope that answers what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever become so focused on something that you can't stop thinking about it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think so, at least not in a way that feels like I can’t stop. I mean, if I’m working on something I care about, I might think about it for a while, but I wouldn’t say I get stuck on it. \n\nUnless you mean something more specific? I just don’t want to answer it wrong.\n\nDoctor: Have you always experienced difficulties with attention and restlessness in multiple settings, such as both at work and at home, throughout your life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t want to overstate it, because I did get by, but it goes back a long way.\n\nEven in grade school, before I was 12, teachers would tell my mother I was bright, but careless. I’d lose homework, forget instructions, or just be sitting there staring out the window instead of finishing my seatwork.\n\nSo yes, it wasn’t just recent or only at work. Does that answer what you mean?\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you ever find yourself intensely focused on a task for a long period of time, to the point where you forget about everything else?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 57 / carpenter / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so… not like forgetting everything else. I can get interested in something, sure, especially if it’s hands-on, but I don’t usually feel like I’m locked in for hours and the world disappears.

If anything, I’d probably be the opposite sometimes, but—sorry, I don’t want to go off track. I haven’t really noticed that happening to me. Does that answer it?

Even when I know I’m supposed to just stay put and finish. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Did you have difficulty paying attention or sitting still in school when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. My moods feel pretty tied to what’s going on, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Did your difficulty with attention and restlessness start when you were a child or did it develop later in life?\n\nPatient: I think it started when I was a kid, yes. So… I guess it wasn’t something that only came on later in life. I don’t like saying that, because I do try hard and I don’t want to sound careless, but it has caused problems.\n\nAt work, if I miss something or get behind, it can turn into rework on a job, and I’ve lost commissions because of mistakes or delays. The crew doesn’t always want to rely on me for things like scheduling or ordering, because I might forget a detail or not get it done when I meant to.\n\nAnd at home… my husband has had to take over a lot of the bills and reminders, because I misplace papers or miss what needs to be done. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess I’m here because I’m having a really hard time keeping my mind on one thing. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but at work especially, one saw starting up, someone walking past the office, or even just a thought about another job can pull me right off what I’m doing. Then I look up and the whole day’s gotten away from me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I try not to be too fidgety or anything, but yes. If I have to sit at the desk doing estimates, or sit through a long meeting, I get this wound-up feeling inside, like I really need to get up and move around. Even when I know I’m supposed to just stay put and finish. My wrist is healed now, so it’s not that—I just feel restless sitting there.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so, not really. I mean, I can make quick decisions sometimes, but I wouldn’t say I’m the kind of person who just goes and does things without thinking. At least, I don’t think so. If there’s a better way to judge that, you can tell me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I’m especially talkative… at least not more than other people. I might talk a bit if I’m comfortable or if it’s about work, but I don’t think people would say I dominate conversations or anything. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble completing tasks or projects once you've started them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I do finish things eventually, usually, but lately it’s been really hard to stay with one thing long enough to get it done. Like if I’m in the office doing paperwork or estimates, one saw starts up, or someone walks by, or I suddenly think of another job I need to check on, and I’m off doing that instead.\n\nThen I come back and I’ve lost my place, and… the whole day can kind of get away from me. I don’t want it to sound like I’m careless or anything, but that’s honestly a lot of what made me come in. I just can’t seem to keep my mind on one thing the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Were you more physically active or restless than your peers when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure about as a child, honestly… I don’t remember being the kid who couldn’t sit still more than everyone else. But now, if I have to sit at the desk doing estimates or sit through a long meeting, I get this sort of wound-up feeling inside, like I really need to get up and move around. Even when I know I’m supposed to just stay put and finish. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Did you have difficulty paying attention or sitting still in school when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it was, because I did okay in school, but even back in grade school—before I was 12—teachers would tell my mother I was bright, but kind of careless.\n\nI’d lose homework, or forget what instructions they’d just given, and I remember staring out the window instead of finishing my seatwork. So… yes, I guess paying attention was a problem even then. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have strong mood swings, where you feel extremely happy, angry, or sad for no apparent reason?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I get frustrated or down like anyone does, especially when I’m overwhelmed, but I wouldn’t say I have these big swings where I’m suddenly extremely happy or angry or sad for no reason.\n\nAt least, I don’t think so. My moods feel pretty tied to what’s going on, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Did your difficulty with attention and restlessness start when you were a child or did it develop later in life?\n\nPatient: I think it started when I was a kid, yes. I didn’t really see it that way then, and I still don’t want to overstate it, but even in grade school—before I was 12—teachers would tell my mother I was bright, just careless.\n\nI’d lose homework, forget instructions they’d just given, or end up staring out the window instead of finishing my work. So… I guess it wasn’t something that only came on later in life. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself so focused on a task that you lose track of time and forget about everything else around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… not in a dramatic way, I don’t think.\n\nI can get absorbed in something, especially if I’m trying to do it right, but I don’t know that I forget the whole world or anything. Maybe I lose track of time a little here and there, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with attention and restlessness caused you any problems in your relationships or daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has, unfortunately. I don’t like saying that, because I do try hard and I don’t want to sound careless, but it has caused problems.\n\nAt work, if I miss something or get behind, it can turn into rework on a job, and I’ve lost commissions because of mistakes or delays. The crew doesn’t always want to rely on me for things like scheduling or ordering, because I might forget a detail or not get it done when I meant to.\n\nAnd at home… my husband has had to take over a lot of the bills and reminders, because I misplace papers or miss what needs to be done. I feel bad about that. I’m not trying to put it on him. It just… keeps happening. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with attention and restlessness at home, or is it mainly a problem at work?\n\nPatient: It’s both, I think… not just work. Work is where it shows up more obviously, because mistakes can cost money — like I’ve lost commissions, or caused rework on jobs when I miss something or get delayed. And I think the crew has gotten less willing to count on me for scheduling or ordering, which is hard to admit.\n\nBut at home it happens too. My husband has had to take over a lot of the bills and reminders because I’ll misplace papers or miss what needs to be done. I don’t mean to make extra work for him, but… yes, it’s definitely not only at work.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had difficulty with attention and restlessness in both work and home settings, or has it varied over time?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there a long time, honestly… though I don’t want to make it sound like I was a terrible student or anything. I did okay in some ways, and people said I was bright.\n\nBut even back in grade school, before I was 12, teachers would tell my mother things like I was careless, or that I’d lose homework, forget instructions, or just be staring out the window instead of finishing my work. So… I guess it wasn’t just something that started at work or at home as an adult. It’s kind of followed me, just showing up differently over time. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with attention and restlessness caused problems in your personal relationships, such as with your husband or friends?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly with my husband, I’d say. I mean, he’s very patient with me, so I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but I know it wears on him.\n\nHe’s had to take over bills and reminders because I keep misplacing papers or missing what needs to be done, and that’s not really fair to him. I feel embarrassed about it, honestly. With work it’s more obvious — mistakes and delays have cost me commissions or caused rework, and the crew doesn’t always trust me with scheduling or ordering now — but at home it does affect us too.\n\nI don’t think I’m trying to be difficult. It just keeps creating extra strain. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been fired or had to quit a job because of your difficulties with attention and restlessness?\n\nPatient: Not fired, no… not that I can say. And I don’t think I’ve had to quit because of it either.\n\nI’ve had some rough stretches at work, and I worry about how I’m seen, but I’ve managed to keep working. I hope that answers what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever become so focused on something that you can't stop thinking about it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think so, at least not in a way that feels like I can’t stop. My wrist is healed now, so it’s not that—I just feel restless sitting there.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so, not really. I mean, I can make quick decisions sometimes, but I wouldn’t say I’m the kind of person who just goes and does things without thinking. At least, I don’t think so. If there’s a better way to judge that, you can tell me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble completing tasks or projects once you've started them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I do finish things eventually, usually, but lately it’s been really hard to stay with one thing long enough to get it done. Like if I’m in the office doing paperwork or estimates, one saw starts up, or someone walks by, or I suddenly think of another job I need to check on, and I’m off doing that instead.\n\nThen I come back and I’ve lost my place, and… the whole day can kind of get away from me. I don’t want it to sound like I’m careless or anything, but that’s honestly a lot of what made me come in. I just can’t seem to keep my mind on one thing the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Were you more physically active or restless than your peers when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure about as a child, honestly… I don’t remember being the kid who couldn’t sit still more than everyone else. I feel bad about that. I’m not trying to put it on him. It just… keeps happening.
